# Consolidation Overhaul Design

This document records the maintainability overhaul plan for reducing duplicated
surface area in Idiom without changing the model/harness authority boundary.
The model continues to own strategy. The harness continues to own contracts,
evidence, permissions, audit, and verification.

The work is intentionally incremental. Each consolidation must first prove the
current behavior with focused tests or characterization fixtures, then move one
owned concept behind a narrower contract. Line-count reduction is useful only
when it removes duplicated authority or repeated coordination logic.

After the first coding-rule attempt, this plan treats net new production surface
as a failing signal unless the patch immediately deletes a larger duplicated
owner. Tests may grow when they characterize existing behavior, but production
code must have a factual before/after ledger for each accepted case.

## Current Baseline

The initial scan used the current worktree as the source of truth. The worktree
already contained user changes in runtime, workspace, coding-rule, and TUI
files, so the implementation plan must preserve those edits.

Measured Go size outside `testdata`:

- Production Go: 331,529 LOC.
- Test Go: 277,845 LOC.
- Go files: 983.

Largest consolidation zones:

- `internal/runtime/agent` and `internal/runtime/workerruntime`: about 78k
  production LOC and 86k test LOC.
- `internal/capabilities/workspace`: about 31k production LOC and 22k test LOC.
- `internal/surfaces/cli` plus `internal/surfaces/tui`: about 130k Go LOC.
- `internal/contracts` plus `internal/workflows`: about 143k Go LOC.

The initial target was several thousand lines of direct reduction without a
large rewrite. Current reviewer-backed estimates below are stricter: broad
early estimates are treated as hypotheses until characterization tests and
reviewer acceptance prove a safe deletion path. The higher-value outcome remains
fewer places that define the same tool, phase, route, evidence, rule, or command
meaning.

## Program Rules

Every case below follows the same sequence:

1. Identify the single owner for the concept.
2. Add or confirm behavior tests before changing the implementation.
3. Move callers to the owner through the smallest useful interface.
4. Keep prompt-facing text, wire fields, audit event names, and error strings
   stable unless a test explicitly approves the change.
5. Run focused tests for the touched boundary, then `go test ./...` and
   `go build ./cmd/idiom` before considering the case complete.
6. Review with the smallest reviewer set that matches the risk.
7. Record production LOC added/removed separately from tests and docs.

Required reviewer lenses:

- Architecture Boundary Reviewer for every case.
- Go Idiom / Package Reviewer when packages or exported APIs move.
- Contract Constants / Enum Reviewer when tool names, phase IDs, action
  classes, rule IDs, event fields, or JSON keys move.
- State Machine / Lifecycle Reviewer for runtime phase, recovery, or session
  work.
- Test Architecture Reviewer when fixture shape or large tests change.
- Quality Attribute Reviewer when privacy, audit, side effects, or release
  posture can change.

## Historical First Implementation Order

This was the initial order used to start the work. It is retained as history;
the current source of truth is the implementation ledger and reviewer-updated
estimate below.

Start with lower-risk authority consolidation before broad runtime rewrites:

1. Coding-rule effective policy API. This area is already isolated and has a
   narrow rule vocabulary.
2. Autonomy audit finding table. The behavior is contract-heavy, but the
   repeated shape is localized.
3. Semantic language detection. This is a low-risk net-negative refactor with
   repeated filesystem walk logic.
4. Workspace tool metadata projections. This prepares the higher-risk runtime
   tool-surface policy work.
5. Runtime profile semantics for TUI route projection.
6. Evidence registry/read-model completion.
7. Plan-execute phase/recovery state table.

The first cases should prove the workflow: tests first, small structural
move, reviewer pass, focused verification, then broad verification.

## Scoped Upcoming Cases

### Tool Surface Policy

Historical read-only exploration found about 1,228 nonblank, non-comment
production lines in duplicated candidate ranges, but the later reviewer pass
rejected that as a safe immediate deletion estimate. The current factual
immediate reduction estimate is `0` production LOC under the existing import
boundaries; the 650-900 LOC figure is retained only as an early scan result.

Potential owner if reopened: strengthen `internal/capabilities/toolcatalog` as the neutral
tool-surface catalog. It should own tool metadata, policy capabilities, side
effects, action class, default/profile visibility, planner allowance,
MCP/discovery projection flags, and unsupported-tool suggestion inventory.

Keep execution, request examples, JSON schemas, and adapter-specific rendering
in `internal/capabilities/workspace`. Keep plan-execute verification command
families in `internal/contracts/plan`; only route runner/workspace shell
exceptions through that contract where they are specifically planner-owned
verification behavior.

Characterization tests required before edits:

- Golden current tool sets for default, write, plan-then-execute planner,
  discovery, MCP discovery, runtime read-only investigation, guarded write,
  and review profiles.
- Parity tests for workspace action class projection, compiled capabilities,
  runner fallback capability/side-effect, planner metadata, and autonomy policy
  events carrying the projected action class.
- Unsupported-tool tests pinning suggestion text, typed failure code/reason,
  and sidecar `unsupported_tool`.
- Native/textual `submit_plan` tests pinning native required mode, textual
  fallback, stringified edits repair, and unsupported transport
  classification.
- Shell verification tests pinning accepted/rejected command families across
  plan extraction, planner rejection, runner rejection, and runtime
  side-effect classification.

Primary risks: import cycles, wire-visible drift in tool/MCP/schema surfaces,
over-consolidating generic shell policy with plan-execute verification policy,
and erasing runtime contextual exceptions such as patch destructiveness and
deliverable-vs-source writes.

### CLI/Desktop Command Metadata

Historical read-only exploration found about 257 LOC of direct duplicated
command/desktop metadata, or about 315 LOC including generic wrappers and
adjacent product transition duplication. The later CLI/Desktop reviewer found
`0` safe immediate production LOC reduction because the root dispatch switch,
Rust validator, TypeScript quick actions, and demo summaries encode different
contracts. The 160-260 LOC figure is retained only as an early scan result.

Potential owner if reopened: do not reintroduce a pure
`internal/contracts/commandmeta` package only to classify telemetry names. Root
dispatch owns dispatch-derived telemetry because it is the source of truth for
which CLI command actually ran. A future shared command manifest would need to
be a broader generated/validated contract for command IDs, desktop allow-list
metadata, and optional quick-action metadata. `internal/contracts/lane` should
remain the owner of true lane IDs. Root dispatch runner functions should stay
in `internal/surfaces/cli/root` to avoid contract-to-surface dependency cycles.

Characterization tests required before edits:

- Golden table for every root command and current telemetry name.
- Golden desktop allowed-command list.
- Golden quick-action command/args list, plus a test that every quick action is
  desktop-allowed.
- Golden lane vocabulary split into CLI lane modes and desktop lane presets.

Known current behavior to preserve unless explicitly changed: several
root-dispatched commands currently fall through telemetry name detection and
often report as `tui`; desktop allows lane `atom-spec` while CLI lane parsing
does not. Rust desktop command validation remains a security boundary and must
not be replaced with frontend-only trust.

  prompt evidence, generated-artifact redirects, delegated read-only packet
  construction, and candidate-consumption recovery now consume these facts
  first, with the old `discovery_query` JSON candidate scanner retained only
  as a factless packet fallback until the remaining delegated-read-only

## Review And Regression Protocol

Before each case starts, record:

- The files and symbols in scope.
- Existing tests that already prove behavior.
- New characterization tests needed before refactoring.
- Wire-visible strings, JSON fields, event names, and prompt-facing text that
  must remain stable.
- The exact focused test command.

During implementation:

- Do not combine unrelated cases in one patch.
- Do not change prompt wording, audit schemas, or event names as incidental
  cleanup.
- Prefer adding a typed owner near the contract over creating a global helper.
- Keep compatibility adapters temporary and delete them once call sites move.

After implementation:

- Run the focused package tests named for the case.
- Run `go test ./...`.
- Run `go build ./cmd/idiom`.
- Ask reviewers to inspect the diff against the case's owner boundary, not
  against personal style preferences.

## Completion Criteria

The overhaul is complete only when every case is either implemented and
verified, or explicitly rejected with evidence that consolidation would increase
complexity or risk. Completion requires:

- A single owner for each accepted duplicated concept.
- Behavior tests covering the moved logic before and after the refactor.
- Reviewer findings resolved or documented with rationale.
- Focused tests, `go test ./...`, and `go build ./cmd/idiom` passing.
- Documentation updated where ownership or contributor workflow changed.
